=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
Mr. Ball and Mr. Box will play a game with balls and boxes.
Initially, Mr. Ball has 10^{100} balls of each of M different types, and Mr. Box has 10^{100} yen.
There are N boxes, where the i-th box has capacity V_i and costs P_i yen. During the game, Mr. Box can buy any box at any time.
In this game, the following operations are repeated until the game ends:

- Mr. Ball chooses one ball and gives it to Mr. Box.
- Mr. Box either accepts the ball or ends the game without accepting it.
- If Mr. Box accepts the ball, he chooses one of his purchased boxes and puts the ball in it.
- If the box with the ball satisfies the following conditions, Mr. Box receives 1 yen. Otherwise, the game ends.
- The number of balls in the box does not exceed its capacity.
- All balls in the box are of the same type.



Mr. Ball will play optimally to minimize Mr. Box's final money, while Mr. Box will play optimally to maximize it.
How much will Mr. Box's money increase throughout the game?
Here, both players have access to all information. In particular, Mr. Ball can see the capacity, price, and contents (type and number of balls) of each box.
Also, note that Mr. Box's initial money is large enough that he will never run out of money to buy boxes.
Solve T test cases for each input file.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format, where \mathrm{case}_i represents the i-th test case:
T
\mathrm{case}_1
\mathrm{case}_2
\vdots
\mathrm{case}_T

Each test case is given in the following format:
N M
V_1 P_1
V_2 P_2
\vdots
V_N P_N

Output

Print the difference between Mr. Box's final and initial money when both players play optimally.

Constraints


- 1\le T,N,M\le 3\times 10^5
- 1\le V_i,P_i \le 10^9
- The sum of N over the T test cases is at most 3\times 10^5.
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

3
3 2
1 1000000000
3 1
3 1
1 300000
1000000000 1
10 4
22 5
26 45
72 21
47 39
97 2
75 35
82 24
17 46
32 22
28 67

Sample Output 1

2
0
28

In the first test case, there are two types of balls and three boxes.
Let us call the two types of balls white and black balls, and call the i-th box box i.
Here is an example of how the game could proceed where the money increases by 2 yen.

- Mr. Ball chooses and gives a white ball.
- Mr. Box accepts the ball, buys box 2 for 1 yen, and puts the white ball in it.
- Box 2 contains 1 white ball. This satisfies the conditions, so Mr. Box receives 1 yen.


- Mr. Ball chooses and gives a white ball.
- Mr. Box accepts the ball and puts it in box 2.
- Box 2 contains 2 white balls. This satisfies the conditions, so Mr. Box receives 1 yen.


- Mr. Ball chooses and gives a black ball.
- Mr. Box accepts the ball, buys box 3 for 1 yen, and puts the black ball in it.
- Box 3 contains 1 black ball. This satisfies the conditions, so Mr. Box receives 1 yen.


- Mr. Ball chooses and gives a white ball.
- Mr. Box accepts the ball and puts it in box 2.
- Box 2 contains 3 white balls. This satisfies the conditions, so Mr. Box receives 1 yen.


- Mr. Ball chooses and gives a white ball.
- Mr. Box chooses to end the game without accepting it.

Finally, box 2 contains 3 white balls and box 3 contains 1 black ball.
Mr. Box spent 2 yen and received 4 yen, so his money increased by 2 yen.
In the second test case, Mr. Ball can play in a way that prevents Mr. Box from earning any money.

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.


Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Maybe because Box can buy boxes at any time, the only relevant information about a type is its current remaining capacity r. Types with r=0 are "new/exhausted"; types with r>0 are "open". Ball can choose:
- an open type with r>0: Box gains 1, r decreases by 1, game continues.
- a zero type: Box may buy a box i (if any) and assign to it, gaining 1, creating r=V_i-1, paying P_i; or stop.
If Ball chooses an open type, Box cannot stop (accept dominates). If Ball chooses zero type, Box can stop; if continues, he selects a box.

This is an impartial game with "resources" boxes. Since all zero types are equivalent except M count; open types have capacities. Ball can introduce zero types until M exhausted? A type can be opened multiple times with different boxes. The number of zero types initially is M, and after an open type's r hits 0, it returns to zero. So there are always M types, each either zero or open with some r. Ball can choose any zero type; if all types open with r>0, Ball must choose an open type (redeem). If there is at least one zero type, Ball can force a purchase/stop.

Let's formalize under this strategy.

State:
- z = number of zero types.
- active capacities multiset A (size M - z), each >0.
- available boxes.

If z > 0:
Ball offers zero. Box may stop (profit 0 additional) or choose an available box i, pay P_i, gain 1, assign to a zero type. If V_i = 1, z unchanged, A unchanged (but box consumed, net 1-P_i ≤0, never optimal). If V_i > 1, z--, add capacity V_i - 1 to A. Then continue with Ball.
If z = 0:
Ball consumes active capacities. Let m = min A. He consumes one type with capacity m for m turns, gaining m rewards, then that type capacity becomes 0, so z=1, A = A \ {m} (remove that capacity; other capacities unchanged). Then continue.

Box's total profit = sum over bought boxes (1 - P_i) + sum over active phase rewards (m each cycle) + also the initial active phase reward after opening all types? Wait the first ball placed in a new box is counted in 1 - P_i, and the remaining capacity V_i - 1 is added to A. During active phase, consuming m rewards corresponds to balls placed in existing boxes beyond the first? Let's check sample1: Buy two boxes: each immediate reward 1, costs 1, net 0 per box. A={2,2}. Active phase m=2: consume two balls, reward2. Total profit2. Yes.

If a type is exhausted and Box buys a new box for it, immediate reward 1 - P, add V-1. Then active phase m = min(new A). So each cycle after initial: buy one box (net 1-P), add capacity V-1, then active reward m = min(A after adding). But note the exhausted type had capacity 0; other active types have remaining capacities from before. The new A includes old capacities (minus the exhausted one removed) plus new V-1. The active phase consumes the smallest capacity in A, which could be an old capacity or the new one. After consuming it, that type becomes zero. So the process continues.

This is reminiscent of a priority queue process. Box can choose which box to buy at each zero challenge. Initially, to reach z=0, he must buy M boxes (one for each type). Since all types zero, each purchase adds one active capacity and reduces z. The order doesn't matter for A; after M purchases, A has M capacities (V_i-1 for chosen boxes). Then active phase consumes min. After that, one capacity removed, one zero type. Then Box can buy another box (for that zero type), adding a capacity, then active phase consumes min, etc. So the game can be seen as:
- Box selects an initial multiset of M boxes to open all types. Cost/reward for each: net c_i = 1 - P_i (could be negative). Then active reward m_0 = min of their capacities a_i = V_i - 1.
- Then for each subsequent cycle, Box selects one additional box (not used) to replenish the exhausted type. It adds capacity a, net c. Then active reward m = min(current active capacities including new a). The exhausted capacity from previous min is removed before adding? Let's track:
After active phase, the type with min capacity m is removed (capacity 0). Other capacities remain. Then Box buys a new box for that zero type, adding capacity a. So active set size remains M. Then active phase consumes new min. Thus after initial, the active set is always of size M (one capacity per type), and each cycle replaces one capacity (the one that just hit zero) with a new capacity chosen by Box. The active reward for the cycle is the minimum capacity in the new active set.

Therefore, after the initial opening of M types, the game is: There are M "slots" (types), each has a remaining capacity. At each cycle, the slot with minimum capacity is "broken" (after yielding its capacity as reward), and Box chooses an unused box to replace it with new capacity a=V-1, receiving net c=1-P, then the cycle reward is the new minimum among all slots. Box can stop at any zero challenge instead of buying a replacement; if he stops, no further costs/rewards. He wants maximize total profit.

But wait: At the initial phase, before z=0, Box buys M boxes. If he stops before M, game ends with profit sum of c for boxes bought (no active rewards). Since c_i = 1 - P_i ≤0 (because P_i≥1), stopping before M yields ≤0. Box can always stop immediately with 0. Thus to get positive profit, he must complete initial M openings. If M > N, impossible to open all types, value 0? If M > N, Box cannot buy M boxes, so z never reaches 0; Ball keeps offering zero, Box eventually stops. Profit from buying boxes with c≤0 is ≤0, so value 0. But wait if some P_i=1, c=0, profit 0; no positive. So value 0. Is that always? Thus if M > N, answer 0. But N,M up to 3e5; M can be >N. If M <= N, Box must choose M boxes to open all types. The net from initial purchases is sum c_i for chosen M boxes, which is ≤0. Active reward m_0 = min a_i among chosen. Total after initial active phase = sum c_i + m_0. Then cycles.

This looks like a "maximum total reward" problem where Box can choose boxes adaptively. Since c_i are nonpositive, but active rewards can be positive. Box wants maximize total. This is similar to maintaining M slots and replacing the minimum each cycle with a new box; each replacement has cost -c_i (since c negative) and yields future minimum rewards. We need compute optimal total value.

Let's define for each box i:
a_i = V_i - 1 ≥0 (remaining capacity after first ball)
c_i = 1 - P_i ≤0 (net immediate profit when buying the box and placing first ball)
If a_i = 0 (V=1), then buying it doesn't create active capacity; it also doesn't reduce z. Such boxes are never useful for opening types or replacing, because they don't change active set and have c≤0. They could be used if P=1 and c=0, but no benefit. So ignore a_i=0.

We need choose an initial set S of size M (since M<=N) to create M active capacities. Then active reward = min_{j in S} a_j. Then we have remaining boxes as replacements. At each cycle, the slot with minimum a is replaced by a chosen remaining box. The reward from that cycle is the new minimum. The net from replacement is c_new. We can continue indefinitely until no boxes or stop. Since all c≤0, Box will only replace if the expected future gain from the replacement (increase in active rewards) outweighs the cost. This is like we have a multiset of M "machines" with lifetimes a; each cycle the shortest lifetime fails, yielding its lifetime as reward, and we can buy a new machine with lifetime a and cost -c to replace it. We want maximize total reward minus cost. This is a classic "maximize profit with M machines, replace shortest lifetime" problem. Since each replacement's cost is paid once and it contributes to future minimums until it is the shortest and fails. We can choose initial machines and replacements.

Maybe there is a simpler interpretation: The total profit over the whole game equals sum over all balls accepted - costs. Under Ball's zero strategy, the game consists of a sequence of "rounds" each ending with a type exhausting. The active reward in each round is the minimum remaining capacity among all M types at the start of the round. After buying a replacement, the minimum may increase. This is analogous to maintaining a set of M values; each step:
- We receive m = min A.
- We remove one occurrence of m (the failing type).
- We may choose a new box with value a and cost d = P - 1 (since c=1-P, cost net? Let's define cost of replacement as q_i = P_i - 1 ≥0, immediate net = -q_i). We add a to A.
- We can stop before replacement, ending after receiving m? Actually after active phase, Box receives m rewards. Then Ball offers zero. If Box stops, game ends. If he buys replacement, he pays q_i (net -q_i) and adds a, then next active phase. So profit per cycle including replacement = m - q_i + future.
Initial phase: choose M boxes, pay q_i each (net -q_i), then receive m_0 = min initial A. So initial profit = m_0 - sum_{initial} q_i.

This is clearer. Each box has cost q_i = P_i - 1 ≥0 and lifetime a_i = V_i - 1 ≥0. Buying a box costs q_i (in addition to the first ball reward cancels P? Wait P_i cost, first ball reward 1, net cost q_i = P_i -1. Yes.)

We need choose initial M boxes (one per type) and then replacements. The total profit = sum of active rewards (minimums) - sum of q_i for all bought boxes.

Since q_i ≥0, buying boxes is costly; active rewards are benefits. We can stop to avoid costs. Need maximize total profit.

Now need solve optimization for large N,M.

Let's formalize the process with M slots. We have a multiset of available boxes with (a_i, q_i). q_i = P_i - 1, a_i = V_i - 1. Ignore a_i=0? They have q≥0, buying gives no lifetime and costs; never useful. So consider a_i>0.

So assume at least M boxes with a>0.

After initial, we have M active lifetimes. The process:
- Let A be multiset of M lifetimes.
- At each cycle, we gain m = min A, then remove one min.
- Then choose an unused box (a,q) to add, pay q. Or stop.
We want maximize total profit = sum of m over cycles - sum q over bought boxes (including initial and replacements).

This is reminiscent of "replacement of shortest lifetime" where the total reward from a box depends on how long it remains as minimum. We can think of each box's lifetime a contributes to the minimums of cycles until it is removed. The active reward in a cycle is the smallest lifetime among current slots. If we sort lifetimes, the minimum is the smallest. Replacing the smallest with a larger lifetime can increase future minimums. This is exactly the process of maintaining a min-heap of size M; each cycle pop min m, gain m, then push new a, pay q. We can choose initial heap and push sequence. We can stop anytime. Since q≥0, we will only push if it increases total future profit.

This looks like we can model each box as an "item" with value a and cost q. The process with a heap of size M: repeatedly pop min, gain its value, then optionally insert an item. So beneficial if a is large enough to raise the minimum. Thus we should only replace the minimum with a box having a greater than the current minimum? Actually if a is between m and second min, new min = a > m, so reward increases from m to a. If a > second min, new min = second min > m. So any a > m increases next reward. Cost q may or may not be worth.

Now we need solve max over B of:
Let s = |B| ≥ M. K = s - M + 1.
Profit = sum of K smallest a in B - sum_{i in B} q_i.

Equivalently, for each box in B, if it is among the K smallest a, it contributes a_i - q_i; if it is among the largest M-1 a, it contributes -q_i. Since K = s - M + 1, among B of size s, exactly K boxes are "rewarded" (popped) and M-1 boxes are "survivors" (not popped). The rewarded boxes are the K smallest a in B. Thus:
Profit(B) = ∑_{i in B} (a_i - q_i) - ∑_{j in survivors} a_j, where survivors are the M-1 largest a in B.
Or = ∑_{i in rewarded} (a_i - q_i) - ∑_{j in survivors} q_j.

We need choose any subset B with |B| ≥ M. Let s vary. This is like selecting items with two attributes; the reward status depends on rank by a within B.

Since survivors are the M-1 largest a in B. So B consists of:
- R: rewarded boxes, size K = s - M + 1.
- S: survivors, size M-1.
All boxes in R have a ≤ all boxes in S? Because rewarded are K smallest, survivors are M-1 largest. Thus max a in R ≤ min a in S (with ties possible). So there is a threshold: choose some boxes as survivors with large a, and rewarded boxes with a ≤ survivor a.

For a fixed threshold x, we can decide which boxes are survivors (a ≥ x) and which are rewarded (a ≤ x). But a box with a exactly x can be either.

Profit = ∑_{i in R} (a_i - q_i) - ∑_{j in S} q_j.

Equivalently, total profit = ∑_{i in B} (a_i - q_i) - ∑_{j in S} a_j.

This is a classic DP/selection problem: We scan boxes in descending a. We maintain how many survivors selected so far (0..M-1). Thus for each possible p (the position of the last survivor), the survivors are chosen from prefix 1..p, with p selected and exactly M-2 others selected from 1..p-1. The condition for rewarded boxes is a ≤ min survivor a (or ≤ last survivor's a). If we choose survivors not including some high-a box, that high-a box cannot be rewarded because its a is larger than a survivor, so it would have been a survivor if selected. Thus rewarded boxes are those with a ≤ the minimum a among survivors? Wait survivors are M-1 largest a in B. The rewarded are K smallest a in B. Therefore every rewarded box has a ≤ every survivor box. So the threshold is the maximum a among rewarded? Actually max rewarded a ≤ min survivor a. If there are ties, equal allowed. So rewarded boxes cannot have a greater than any survivor. Thus if a box has a > min survivor a, it cannot be rewarded; if selected, it must be a survivor. Therefore, to have a rewarded box with high a, we need all M-1 survivors to have a at least that high. So the survivor set should consist of boxes with large a; the minimum survivor a is the threshold.

This is better formulated by threshold t = min a among survivors. Then:
- Survivors: M-1 boxes with a ≥ t, chosen to minimize q (since contribution -q).
- Rewarded: any boxes with a ≤ t? Wait rewarded must have a ≤ every survivor, so a ≤ t. But if a box has a = t, it can be either survivor or rewarded depending on tie. If a < t, rewarded. If a > t, cannot be rewarded.
- Additionally, rewarded boxes must be selected if w_i = a - q > 0. Since they don't affect survivors, select all with positive w among a ≤ t (and maybe some with a=t not used as survivors).
- The total number of rewarded boxes K can be any; profit = -sum q_survivors + sum_{rewarded, a≤t, w>0} w.

But is it always optimal to select all rewarded boxes with positive w and a ≤ t? Yes, because adding a rewarded box increases profit by w and doesn't change survivor set or threshold (it has a ≤ t, so remains among K smallest; K increases by 1, s increases by1, formula still holds). It may require buying an extra box and getting one extra reward; if w>0, beneficial. If w=0, indifferent; ignore.

What about rewarded boxes with a > t? Cannot be rewarded because they would be larger than some survivor; if selected, they'd be survivors, increasing survivor count beyond M-1. But we could choose them as survivors instead of some lower-q? Survivor count fixed M-1; if we select an additional high-a box, it would be a survivor, but then we would have M survivors, not allowed. The formula requires exactly M-1 survivors (largest a in B). If B has more than M-1 boxes with a > t, then the survivors would be the M-1 largest among them, and the threshold would be higher. So our threshold t is the (M-1)-th largest a in B. Thus there are exactly M-1 selected boxes with a ≥ t (including ties). Boxes with a > t that are not selected as survivors cannot be in B at all (because if selected, they'd be among top M-1 and increase survivor count). So we should not select them as rewarded.

Therefore, for a threshold t (integer value of a), the optimal B is:
- Choose M-1 boxes with a ≥ t as survivors, minimizing total q. (These are the M-1 largest a in B; if there are more than M-1 boxes with a > t, we must choose the M-1 largest? Actually if we choose survivors with a ≥ t but there are boxes with a > t not selected, then the M-1 largest a in B might include some unselected? No, B only contains selected survivors and rewarded. If we skip a box with a > t, it's not in B. The survivors are selected boxes with largest a. If we select a survivor with a = t and skip a box with a > t, that's allowed; the skipped box not in B. The threshold t is min survivor a. So survivors can be any M-1 boxes with a ≥ t, not necessarily the absolute largest available. We choose them to minimize q.)
- Rewarded: all boxes with a < t and w > 0, plus possibly boxes with a = t that are not survivors and w > 0. But if we select a box with a = t as rewarded, then there are survivors with a = t; max rewarded a = t, min survivor a = t, allowed. If we select too many boxes with a = t as rewarded, the M-1 largest a in B are still the survivors (since survivors have a ≥ t and rewarded have a ≤ t; if more than M-1 boxes with a = t selected, some with a=t would be among largest M-1, so survivor set would include some of them. But we already have M-1 survivors; selecting additional a=t boxes as rewarded means total selected with a=t > number of survivors with a=t? Then the M-1 largest a in B would include some of the rewarded a=t boxes instead of maybe survivors with a>t? Wait if survivors include boxes with a>t, they are definitely larger. If all M-1 survivors have a=t, and we select additional a=t rewarded, then there are >M-1 boxes with a=t and no larger survivors; the M-1 largest a in B are some M-1 of the a=t boxes, and the remaining a=t boxes are rewarded. This is okay; we can designate which a=t boxes are survivors. The threshold t remains. So we can select any number of a=t boxes as rewarded, as long as we also select at least M-1 boxes with a≥t as survivors; if total selected with a≥t exceeds M-1, the survivors are the M-1 largest among them, which may include some a=t. The rewarded a=t boxes are those not among top M-1. For profit, survivors contribute -q, rewarded contribute w. If a box has a=t and w>0, we would prefer it to be rewarded rather than survivor (since rewarded gives w instead of -q; w = a-q, -q = w-a, so rewarded is larger by a). But survivor count fixed; we choose M-1 boxes with largest a to be survivors. Among equal a, we can choose which are survivors to minimize q (or maximize profit). So for a=t, we need decide how many of the selected a=t boxes are survivors vs rewarded. This can be handled by sorting ties carefully or by considering the M-1 largest a in B: if there are boxes with a>t selected as survivors, then all a=t selected can be rewarded. If not enough a>t survivors, some a=t must be survivors.

This is getting complex but manageable with sorting and prefix sums.

Alternative approach: Use the sorted-order DP model. Sort boxes by a descending. We need choose M-1 survivors (first selected) and then rewarded boxes after the last survivor. This automatically handles ties if we define a total order. But if equal a, the order among them can affect which equal-a boxes are survivors/rewarded. We can choose an order that optimizes: among equal a, we can place boxes with high w (a-q) later so they can be rewarded, and boxes with low q earlier as survivors. Since a equal, survivor contribution -q, rewarded contribution a-q. A box with high q is bad as survivor; with low q good as survivor. A box with high w is good as rewarded. Note w = a - q, so for fixed a, lower q means higher w. The same box: if survivor, contribution -q; if rewarded, contribution a-q. Difference = a >0, so every box prefers rewarded over survivor. But we need exactly M-1 survivors. We should choose as survivors the boxes where the opportunity cost of not rewarding them is smallest? Among equal a, survivor contribution -q, rewarded contribution a-q. The loss from making a box survivor instead of rewarded is a (constant for equal a). So for equal a, any box can be survivor; to maximize, choose survivors with smallest q? Since survivor contribution -q, yes choose lowest q as survivors, and reward the rest (if w>0). But if a box has w≤0, we wouldn't reward it; could still be survivor if needed? Survivor contribution -q; if q large, maybe not. But if we need M-1 survivors, we might have to take some negative contribution.

Let's step back. Maybe there is an even simpler formula using sorting by a descending and a priority queue.

From the sorted-order DP:
Sort boxes by a descending. We scan from high a to low a. We need select M-1 survivors. Once we have selected M-1 survivors, any subsequent selected box is rewarded with weight w_i = a_i - q_i. We can skip boxes. We want maximize total weight. This is equivalent to:
- We must choose a cut point in the sequence: before the cut, we select exactly M-1 boxes as survivors (not necessarily contiguous); after the cut, we select any boxes with positive w. The cut is after the last selected survivor. But the last selected survivor's position determines which boxes are "after". Boxes before the cut that are not selected are skipped and cannot be rewarded. Boxes after the cut can be selected if w>0.

Thus for each position p (the index of the last survivor), the optimal:
- Survivors: choose M-1 boxes from prefix p, with p included, minimizing sum q (since contribution -q). (Because p is the last survivor; exactly M-1 survivors in prefix p, and p is one of them. We can choose any M-2 from prefix p-1.)
- Rewarded: all boxes in suffix p+1..N with w_i > 0. (What about boxes at same a as p but after p in tie order? If we sort ties arbitrarily, p is last survivor; boxes after p with same a can be rewarded. This is okay if we choose tie order appropriately; but if we sort ties by something, p's position among equal a matters. We can handle by considering all possible p within tie groups or by sorting ties so that boxes with w>0 come after boxes with low q? We'll address.)

Then profit(p) = - (sum q of chosen M-1 survivors from prefix p including p) + sum_{i>p, w_i>0} w_i.
We want max over p from M-1 to N (need at least M-1 boxes in prefix; if p < M-1 impossible). Also need at least one rewarded box after p? If no rewarded boxes selected, then B size = M-1, K=0, profit = -sum q_survivors ≤0. But we can choose empty for 0. If p=N, suffix empty, profit negative/nonpositive; not optimal unless 0. So p < N likely.

Is this DP correct? Let's test with examples.

p=1: survivors from prefix1 include1 cost0; suffix w>0: 4+3=7; profit7.
p=2: survivors from prefix2 include2? Need choose 1 survivor from prefix2 including p=2. Options survivor=1 or2, min q=0. Suffix w>0: box3 w=3. Profit3. Max7. Good.

But what if optimal survivors are not including the last selected survivor at position p? By definition p is the last survivor, so yes.

What if there are boxes after p with w>0 but a greater than some survivor? Since sorted descending, after p has a ≤ a_p. Survivors include p with a_p, so all after have a ≤ a_p. Good.

What if we skip some boxes before p with negative w and high a; they are not selected, fine. What if a box before p has positive w but we skip it because selecting it before completing M-1 survivors would make it a survivor (bad) or would change p? Actually if we select a box before p as a rewarded box, it would be among first M-1 selected and thus survivor, not rewarded. So we cannot select it as rewarded. It could be selected as a survivor if its q is low. If it has positive w but high q, maybe not good as survivor. So skipping is correct.

Now, the DP requires for each p: minimum sum of q for M-1 survivors from prefix p with p included. This is q_p + sum of the M-2 smallest q among prefix p-1. Let prefMinQ[p-1][M-2] be sum of M-2 smallest q in first p-1. Since M up to 3e5, we can maintain a max-heap of size M-2 while scanning p from M-1 to N? Actually as p increases, we need sum of M-2 smallest q in prefix p-1. We can precompute by scanning and maintaining heap of selected smallest q? But M-2 can be large; we need for each p the sum of k smallest q in prefix p-1 where k = M-2. This is dynamic order statistics. We can compute by processing p from 1 to N, maintaining a max-heap of size k of the k smallest q seen so far, with sum. For p, before adding box p? Need prefix p-1. We can iterate p from 1..N:
- Maintain heap of k smallest q among boxes 1..p-1.
- For p, survivor cost = q_p + heap_sum (if heap size == k; if p-1 < k invalid).
- Then add q_p to heap for next p.
This is O(N log M). Good.

But wait: The survivors chosen from prefix p with p included and M-2 smallest q from prefix p-1. This minimizes sum q for a fixed p. However, is it always valid to choose the M-2 smallest q from prefix p-1 as survivors, even if some have a equal/larger and maybe should be rewarded instead? They are before p, so if selected they are survivors. Fine. They might have q very low but w positive; selecting them as survivors contributes -q, not w. But if we didn't select them as survivors, we would need some other box as survivor (maybe p and others). Since survivor count fixed, choosing lowest q among prefix p is optimal. Could it be better to choose a survivor from after p? No, p is last survivor; all survivors must be before or at p in sorted order. So no.

Now, suffix reward sum: sum_{i>p, w_i>0} w_i. This can be precomputed as suffix sum of positive w.

Then answer = max(0, max_{p} ( - (q_p + sum_k_smallest_q_prefix(p-1)) + suffix_pos_w[p+1] )).

But what if among equal a, we want a box with high q to be rewarded and low q to be survivor? Since rewarded contribution w = a-q, survivor -q. If we choose which boxes in group are survivors, we should choose survivors with smallest q to maximize -sum q_s (since rewarded contribution also decreases with q, but if a box is rewarded, its q is subtracted too; actually total contribution of a box if survivor = -q; if rewarded = a-q. For any box, rewarded is better by a. So we would like as many boxes as possible to be rewarded, but survivor count fixed. Among boxes that must be survivors, choose those with smallest q? Let's compare two boxes x,y with q_x<q_y. If x survivor, y rewarded: total = -q_x + a - q_y = a - q_x - q_y. If y survivor, x rewarded: total = -q_y + a - q_x = same! For equal a, the total is independent of which box is survivor, as long as the rewarded one has w>0? Now, is the suffix positive w sum independent of p? Yes, all boxes after p with w>0 selected. But what if a box after p has a greater than some survivor? Since sorted descending, after p a ≤ a_p = min survivor a (because p is last survivor in sorted order, and survivors are before p, so their a ≥ a_p). So okay.

What if there are boxes after p with w>0 but selecting them would change the survivor set? No, they are after the last survivor, so they are rewarded; adding them doesn't affect the first M-1 selected survivors because survivors are before p. Even if a rewarded box has a equal to a_p and there are already M-1 survivors before p, the M-1 largest a in B are still the survivors (all have a ≥ a_p; rewarded have a ≤ a_p). If there are ties and more than M-1 boxes with a = a_p in B, the M-1 largest could include some rewarded a_p boxes instead of some survivor a_p boxes. But since a equal, the set of largest M-1 by a is not unique; we can designate the survivors as the ones before p. The formula requires survivors are the M-1 largest a in B, but with ties any selection works. The profit calculation using our designation is valid because the active process would pop the K smallest a; if there are ties, the popped set could be any K smallest, and the profit (sum of K smallest a) is the same regardless of which tied boxes are popped, but costs differ? Wait cost is sum q over all B, independent of which are popped. The reward sum is sum of K smallest a values, independent of which boxes. So if there are ties, the designation of which tied boxes are survivors/rewarded doesn't affect reward sum, but it affects cost? Cost is sum q over all selected boxes, independent. The formula Profit = sum of K smallest a in B - sum q over B. This depends only on the multiset of a and q in B, not on which tied boxes are considered survivors. In our DP, we choose B as: survivors set S (M-1 boxes) + rewarded set R (boxes after p with w>0). The K smallest a in B are indeed the rewarded boxes? If some rewarded box has a equal to survivor min, then there are ties; the K smallest a in B are all boxes except the M-1 survivors? Since survivors have a ≥ rewarded. If ties, some survivors have same a as rewarded; the set of K smallest a includes all rewarded and maybe some survivors with same a? Actually K = |B| - M + 1 = |R|. Since |S|=M-1, K=|R|. The K smallest a in B should be exactly R if all a in R ≤ all a in S. If ties, some a in S equal some in R, then the K smallest values are not uniquely R; they could include some S and exclude some R, but the sum of the K smallest a values is still sum_{R} a because all tied values equal. So reward sum = sum_{i in R} a_i. Thus profit = sum_R a_i - sum_{S∪R} q_i = sum_R (a_i - q_i) - sum_S q_i. This matches our DP: rewarded contribution w_i = a_i - q_i, survivor contribution -q_i. Great. So tie designation is fine.

Therefore, the DP seems correct.

But wait: The DP chooses rewarded boxes as all boxes after p with w_i > 0. What if a box after p has w_i > 0 but selecting it would make the number of rewarded boxes K such that the K smallest a in B include some survivor with a smaller than a rewarded? Impossible because survivors are before p, so a_survivor ≥ a_p ≥ a_rewarded. So all rewarded have a ≤ all survivors. Good.

Now, is there any benefit to selecting a box after p with w_i = 0? It adds 0 profit, no effect; can ignore. w_i <0 not selected.

What about selecting a box before p as a rewarded box by making it after p in tie order? That's handled by choosing p after that box. If the box has a greater than p, cannot be after p; if selected, it would be survivor. So if its w is positive but q high, maybe we don't want it as survivor; we skip it. Could we instead choose it as a survivor and drop a lower-a survivor, changing p? The DP over all p covers.

What if there is a box with high a, high q but we still need it as survivor because not enough boxes? Example M=3, only three boxes: A a=100,q=1000; B a=90,q=0; C a=80,q=0. Need 2 survivors. Now, is the DP missing the possibility of choosing more than M-1 survivors? No, exactly M-1.

Let's prove the sorted-order DP from the formula:
For any B, sort its boxes by a descending. The first M-1 are survivors S, the rest R. Let p be the position in the global sorted list of the last survivor (the M-1-th selected box). Then S ⊆ prefix p, p ∈ S, R ⊆ suffix p+1 (with ties after p). For fixed p, to maximize profit:
- Survivor contribution -q; choose S of size M-1 from prefix p including p with minimum q sum.
- Rewarded contribution a-q; choose all boxes in suffix p+1 with positive a-q.
This is exactly DP. Need also consider if there are boxes with same a as p that are in suffix p+1 but have a equal; okay.

Now, complexity O(N log N) for sorting, O(N log M) for heap. N total 3e5, fine.

But wait, M can be up to 3e5, but if M > N, no valid p because need M-1 survivors before p and p ≤ N. Our loop can handle; heap size M-2 may exceed p-1. We'll initialize invalid. If M-1 > N, no p; answer 0. If M=1, M-1=0 survivors. The DP with k = M-2 = -1? Need handle separately.

Let's handle M=1 separately. Formula: survivors count 0, K = s. Profit = sum over B (a - q) = sum (V - P). Box can choose any subset; optimal select boxes with positive V-P. Now, we need be careful about sorting ties. Our DP assumes sorted by a descending. If equal a, any order works? Let's prove. The DP over p with arbitrary tie order should yield correct max because for any optimal B, we can order ties so that the last survivor p is at the position of the M-1-th selected box; boxes in R with same a can be placed after p. If our fixed tie order differs, p may be different but there exists some p in the tie group corresponding to the last survivor. Since we evaluate all p, we should capture it. However, the survivor cost for a given p uses M-2 smallest q from prefix p-1. If tie order is arbitrary, for an optimal B where the last survivor is a particular box, our order might place some rewarded equal-a boxes before that survivor, making them in prefix and thus not selectable as rewarded. But we could instead choose a different p (later in tie group) as last survivor, including those rewarded boxes in suffix? Let's examine.

Suppose equal a group G. In an optimal B, let S_G be survivors from G, R_G rewarded from G. The last survivor in sorted order could be some box in S_G. If our fixed order puts some R_G boxes before some S_G boxes, then the last survivor position might be after those R_G, so they would be before p and not selectable as rewarded. But we could choose p as the last S_G in our order; if R_G boxes before it, then those R_G are in prefix and would be forced to be survivors if selected, which may not match. However, since all a equal, we can swap the identities of survivors and rewarded among G without changing profit? Earlier we found for equal a, total contribution of a group with s survivors and r rewarded is -sum q_S + sum_{R}(a-q). If we choose which boxes are S vs R, the total depends on q of S and R. Let's compute total for all selected boxes in G: contribution = -∑_{S} q + ∑_{R} (a - q) = r*a - ∑_{G_selected} q. This depends only on r (number rewarded) and the total q of all selected boxes in G, not on which are S/R. Since S size fixed M-1 overall, but within G, if we swap a box between S and R, r changes? No, total number of rewarded boxes R = |B| - (M-1). For a fixed B, r is fixed. The contribution of G is r_G * a - sum q of selected G. This is independent of which selected G boxes are survivors vs rewarded. Therefore, for a fixed set of selected boxes, the profit is independent of tie ordering. Thus if our fixed order puts some selected R before S, we can still select the same set B; the DP's designation of survivors as first M-1 selected might differ, but the profit formula using our designation should still yield the same total? Let's check: DP profit = -sum q_S + sum_{R}(a-q). If we swap a box between S and R, the total changes by? Suppose box x in S, y in R, same a. Original contribution: -q_x + a - q_y. Swapped: -q_y + a - q_x. Equal. So yes, any designation of which equal-a selected boxes are survivors/rewarded gives same profit. Thus arbitrary tie order is fine. Now, heap maintenance for k = M-2:
We need for each p from 1 to N:
- If p-1 >= k (i.e., p >= k+1 = M-1), then survivor cost = q_p + heap_sum.
- Then add q_p to heap if k>0.
But careful: heap should contain k smallest q among prefix p-1. We process p increasing. Initially heap empty, sum=0. For p=1, prefix empty. If k=0, heap_sum=0. If k>0 and p-1 < k, invalid. After computing for p, add q_p to heap and maintain size k.

Pseudo:
k = M - 2
heap = []  # max-heap of selected q (store -q)
heap_sum = 0
ans = 0
suffix_pos = array length N+2 maybe precomputed.
First compute suffix_pos[i] = sum of positive w from i to N? We need suffix after p: sum_{i>p} max(0,w_i). Let pos = [0]*(N+2)
for i from N down to 1:
    pos[i] = pos[i+1] + max(0, w_i)  # w_i = a_i - q_i = V-P
Then for p in 1..N:
    if p-1 >= k:  # enough boxes before p to choose k survivors
        cost = q_p + heap_sum
        profit = pos[p+1] - cost
        if profit > ans: ans = profit
    # add q_p to heap
    if k > 0:
        heappush max: if len(heap) < k: push -q_p, heap_sum += q_p
        else if q_p < -heap[0]: replace, heap_sum += q_p - (-heap[0])? Need manage.

Now, let's think about the "all information" and "Mr. Box can buy any box at any time" - our model assumes Box can buy a box exactly when a zero type is offered. If Ball deviates and consumes active, Box might need to buy a box before a zero offer? No, buying before needed not beneficial. Our strategy for Box to achieve formula: follow the DP's B. If Ball offers zero, buy the next box according to the plan (initial M boxes first, then replacements). If Ball offers active, accept. If Ball's deviations cause a type to become zero earlier, Box may need to buy a replacement earlier than planned. But the plan's order of buying boxes can be adjusted: the set B is fixed; the sequence of purchases is determined by which types become zero. Under zero strategy, the purchases are: first M boxes (one per type), then after each active phase, one replacement. If Ball consumes active during initial phase, a type may become zero before all M types opened; then Box may need to buy a replacement for it before opening all types. This means the first M boxes in the plan might not all be "initial" before first active reward; some are replacements. Does the formula still hold for any Ball strategy? Let's also consider Ball's choice in active phase: when z=0, he must consume some active type. To minimize Box profit, he should consume the type with smallest remaining capacity, because that minimizes the number of rewards before a zero appears. If he consumes a larger capacity, Box gets more rewards before zero, increasing profit. So min is optimal. This is intuitive.

Now, let's think about the initial phase if z>0. Ball's zero strategy: always offer zero. Is it always optimal? If he consumes active, Box gets a reward and active capacity decreases, potentially making that type zero sooner, which increases z. Increasing z gives Ball more zero options, but also gave a reward. Could consuming active ever reduce Box's future profit enough to compensate for the reward? Since the reward is +1, and the state after consuming active has one less active capacity. If that capacity was going to be consumed later anyway under zero strategy, then consuming it now just gives the same reward earlier; no loss to Box, and Ball cannot reduce total rewards. If that capacity was a survivor (would never be consumed under zero strategy), consuming it gives extra reward, bad for Ball. If it was going to be consumed later, the total reward is same, but the timing changes; zero challenges may occur differently. Could timing affect Box's ability to buy boxes and profit? Box's costs/rewards are additive; earlier rewards don't hurt Box. Earlier zero creation might force Box to buy replacements earlier, but if Box planned to buy them later, paying earlier doesn't change total cost; if Box would stop instead, he has more rewards. So consuming active cannot reduce Box's total profit. Thus Ball should never consume active while a zero exists. This can be a proof.

Now, let's refine the DP and code.

Data types:
- V_i, P_i up to 1e9, N sum 3e5. a up to 1e9-1, q up to 1e9-1, w up to 1e9-1 (if P=1) or negative down to -1e9. Sums up to 3e14, Python int ok.

Implementation details:
For each test case:
```
import sys, heapq
def solve():
    input = sys.stdin.readline
    T = int(input())
    out = []
    for _ in range(T):
        N, M = map(int, input().split())
        boxes = []
        for i in range(N):
            V,P = map(int, input().split())
            a = V - 1
            q = P - 1
            w = V - P
            boxes.append((a, q, w))
        if M == 1:
            ans = sum(max(0, w) for _,_,w in boxes)
            out.append(str(ans))
            continue
        boxes.sort(key=lambda x: (-x[0], x[1]))  # a desc, q asc
        # suffix positive w
        suf = [0]*(N+2)
        for i in range(N-1, -1, -1):
            w = boxes[i][2]
            suf[i] = suf[i+1] + (w if w > 0 else 0)
        k = M - 2
        heap = []  # max-heap via negative
        heap_sum = 0
        ans = 0
        for p in range(N):
            a,q,w = boxes[p]
            if p >= k:  # p is 0-index, prefix before p has p elements. Need p >= k.
                cost = q + heap_sum
                profit = suf[p+1] - cost
                if profit > ans:
                    ans = profit
            # add q to heap of k smallest in prefix up to p (for next p+1)
            if k > 0:
                if len(heap) < k:
                    heapq.heappush(heap, -q)
                    heap_sum += q
                elif q < -heap[0]:
                    heap_sum += q - (-heap[0])
                    heapq.heapreplace(heap, -q)
        out.append(str(ans))
    print("\n".join(out))
```
Check condition p >= k with 0-index:
- p is index of current box (0-based). Prefix before p has p elements. Need p >= k (k=M-2). For M=2, k=0, p>=0 always. For M=3, k=1, need p>=1 (at least 1 element before p) so p can be index1 (second box) as last survivor with one survivor before. Correct. For M=4, k=2, p>=2 (third box) with two before. Correct.
- Survivor count M-1 = k+1. Need p has k elements before it, so p >= k. Good.

But wait, if k=0, heap remains empty, heap_sum=0. Good.

If you wait, you don't pay q until needed; if the game ends before, you save cost. Buying early cannot improve profit because it only adds cost and capacity that may not be used. However, adding capacity early could raise the minimum and cause Ball to consume a different type, potentially increasing total active rewards. But since Ball controls which type to consume, if you increase a type's capacity, Ball will just consume the new minimum (another type) and ignore the increased type until later. The increased capacity might be used later if that type becomes the minimum; but you could have bought the box later when it becomes zero, with the same effect on future rewards and no risk. So buying early is weakly dominated. Thus optimal Box never buys a box for an active type. Good.

Now, let's consider if T up to 3e5 but sum N 3e5; reading all lines okay. Use sys.stdin.buffer.read for speed. We can parse all ints at once. Since final code should be efficient.

Implementation with fast parser:
```
import sys, heapq

def solve():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    it = iter(data)
    T = next(it)
    out = []
    for _ in range(T):
        N = next(it); M = next(it)
        boxes = []
        for _ in range(N):
            V = next(it); P = next(it)
            boxes.append((V-1, P-1, V-P))
        ...
    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))
```

Now, let's think about the heap maintenance more carefully.

We need for each p, heap contains k smallest q among boxes[0:p] (prefix before p). Initially empty. Loop p from 0 to N-1:
- At start of iteration p, heap has k smallest q among prefix before p. We maintain this invariant.
- If p >= k, valid. cost = q_p + heap_sum.
- After processing p, we need heap for next p+1: k smallest q among prefix before p+1, i.e., prefix including p. So add q_p.

Implementation:
```
if k > 0:
    if len(heap) < k:
        heapq.heappush(heap, -q)
        heap_sum += q
    elif q < -heap[0]:
        heap_sum += q - (-heap[0])
        heapq.heapreplace(heap, -q)
```
This maintains max-heap of k smallest q. If len(heap) < k, push. If heap full and q smaller than current largest among k smallest, replace. If q larger, ignore. Good.

But note: If k=0, heap_sum=0. We skip.

Now, let's test with M=3, k=1, q prefix:
boxes q: [5,1,4]
p=0: heap empty, p>=1 false. add5 heap [5] sum5.
p=1: heap has k smallest among prefix [5] =5. p>=1 true. cost q_p1 +5 =6. add1: heap full, 1<5 replace sum1.
p=2: heap has k smallest among prefix [5,1] =1. cost q_p4 +1=5. Good.

Now, suffix_pos:
```
suf = [0]*(N+2)
for i in range(N-1, -1, -1):
    w = boxes[i][2]
    suf[i] = suf[i+1] + (w if w > 0 else 0)
```
Then suf[p+1] is sum positive w for indices > p. Good.

Potential issue with `data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))` memory: total ints ~ 2*sumN + 2T +1 ≤ 6e5+? If we want lower memory, use sys.stdin.buffer.readline. Simpler and memory safe. N total 3e5, line reading okay. Let's use readline for clarity.

Code:
```
import sys, heapq

def solve():
    input = sys.stdin.readline
    T = int(input())
    out = []
    for _ in range(T):
        N, M = map(int, input().split())
        boxes = []
        for _ in range(N):
            V, P = map(int, input().split())
            boxes.append((V-1, P-1, V-P))
        if M == 1:
            ans = sum(max(0, w) for _,_,w in boxes)
            out.append(str(ans))
            continue
        if M > N:
            out.append("0")
            continue
        boxes.sort(key=lambda x: (-x[0], x[1]))
        suf = [0]*(N+2)
        for i in range(N-1, -1, -1):
            w = boxes[i][2]
            suf[i] = suf[i+1] + (w if w > 0 else 0)
        k = M - 2
        heap = []
        heap_sum = 0
        ans = 0
        for p in range(N):
            a,q,w = boxes[p]
            if p >= k:
                cost = q + heap_sum
                profit = suf[p+1] - cost
                if profit > ans:
                    ans = profit
            if k > 0:
                if len(heap) < k:
                    heapq.heappush(heap, -q)
                    heap_sum += q
                elif q < -heap[0]:
                    heap_sum += q - (-heap[0])
                    heapq.heapreplace(heap, -q)
        out.append(str(ans))
    print("\n".join(out))
```
Wait: If M > N, we skip sorting, but we already stored boxes. Fine.

But there is a subtle bug: In the loop, for p, we compute profit using heap_sum which is k smallest q in prefix before p. Then we add q_p to heap. However, if p < k, invalid, but we still add q_p. This means for p+1, heap contains prefix p. Good. But if k > 0 and len(heap) < k, we push q_p. If p < k, heap size grows. Once p reaches k, heap has k elements (prefix before p? Let's check: At p=k, before processing, we have added boxes 0..k-1, heap size k. Good. Condition p>=k true. Then after processing p=k, we add q_k, heap remains size k. Good.

Now, consider k=0. Condition p>=0 true. We skip heap. Good.

Now, consider M=2, k=0, but we early M>N? If M=2,N=1, M>N true output0. Correct.

Now, consider M=3,N=3,k=1. Loop:
p=0: p>=1 false. add q0 heap size1.
p=1: heap has q0. p>=1 true. cost q1+heap_sum. add q1 replace if smaller.
p=2: heap has min(q0,q1). p>=2 true. cost q2+min. add q2. Good.

Now, let's think about if the survivor cost should include p's q plus k smallest q from prefix p-1, but if p's q is also among the k smallest, we might double count? No, heap_sum is from prefix p-1, not including p. So no double count. Good.

Now, let's think about if the heap should contain k smallest q from prefix p-1, but if k=0, heap_sum=0. Good.

Now, let's test with M=3, boxes q all 0, a desc. k=1. p=1 cost0, suffix sum after p. This corresponds survivors = box0 and box1 (p), rewarded after. Good.

Now, let's think about if sorting by (-a, q) and then suffix_pos sums w for boxes after p. If there are boxes with same a but q ascending, a box with same a and lower q before p could be chosen as survivor, and a box with same a and higher q after p rewarded. This is good. If a box with same a and higher q before p and lower q after p? Sorting q ascending prevents lower q after higher q. So low q before, high q after. If high q before and low q after cannot happen. Is that a problem? Suppose optimal wants high-q box as survivor and low-q box rewarded; for equal a, profit same as low-q survivor high-q rewarded, so no problem. If high-q box has w negative and low-q box w positive, we want low-q rewarded, high-q skipped; sorting q asc gives low-q before, so p at low-q makes low-q survivor not rewarded, but p at high-q? Let's examine M=2, group a=10: X low q=0,w=10; Y high q=100,w=-90. Optimal is X survivor, skip Y, profit0. Sorting q asc: X then Y. p=X: cost0, suffix Y w neg ->0. Good. What if X low q=0,w=-5 (a=5? w=a-q, if q=0 w positive always because a>0. For a=10,q=0 w=10 positive. If a=5,q=0 w=5 positive. Since q>=0 and a>0, w can be negative only if q>a. Low q likely positive. So high-q boxes tend to have lower w. Sorting q asc puts positive w boxes before negative w boxes. For equal a, to reward positive w boxes, we need p before them; but if they are before p, they become survivors. However, as noted, profit independent of which equal-a box is survivor/rewarded if both selected. But if negative w box is skipped, and positive w box is survivor, no reward from it. Let's maybe try to prove the DP formula one more time in a concise way for myself:
For any set B of bought boxes, let s=|B|. Under Ball's strategy:
- Ball always offers a type with no balls if exists; otherwise a type with fewest remaining balls.
- This forces Box to buy boxes only for empty types. Each bought box has "extra capacity" a=V-1 and "net cost" q=P-1.
- The game proceeds in rounds. In each round, the type with smallest remaining capacity is emptied, giving a reward equal to that capacity. Then Box may buy a new box for that type.
- If Box buys exactly B and stops after K rounds, the emptied boxes are the K boxes of B with smallest a. Hence profit = sum of K smallest a in B - sum q over B, where K = |B|-M+1.
- Therefore Box's optimal profit is max over B of that expression.
Then the DP:
Sort boxes by decreasing a. In any optimal B, the M-1 boxes with largest a are never emptied; call them survivors. The last survivor in sorted order is at some position p. All emptied boxes lie after p. For a fixed p, survivors should be p plus the M-2 boxes before p with smallest q; emptied boxes should be all boxes after p with positive a-q. Evaluate all p.

This is solid.

Now, let's think about if "the emptied boxes are the K boxes of B with smallest a" under Ball's min strategy requires that Box buys exactly B and stops after K rounds, but if Box buys a box for an empty type and then Ball consumes a different type, the new box might not be emptied. The set of emptied boxes after K rounds are the K smallest a in B, as proven. Good.

Now, let's consider if the number of rounds K = |B|-M+1. If Box buys B and stops after K active rewards, total bought = M + (K-1) = |B|, so K = |B|-M+1. Yes.

Now, let's think about if Box could buy boxes not in B and then stop, but optimal B is all bought boxes. Good.

Full code:

```
import sys
import heapq

def solve():
    input = sys.stdin.readline
    T = int(input())
    out = []
    for _ in range(T):
        N, M = map(int, input().split())
        if M == 1:
            ans = 0
            for _ in range(N):
                V, P = map(int, input().split())
                if V > P:
                    ans += V - P
            out.append(str(ans))
            continue
        if M > N:
            for _ in range(N):
                input()
            out.append("0")
            continue
        boxes = []
        for _ in range(N):
            V, P = map(int, input().split())
            boxes.append((V - 1, P - 1, V - P))
        boxes.sort(key=lambda x: (-x[0], x[1]))
        suf = [0] * (N + 2)
        for i in range(N - 1, -1, -1):
            w = boxes[i][2]
            suf[i] = suf[i + 1] + (w if w > 0 else 0)
        k = M - 2
        heap = []
        heap_sum = 0
        ans = 0
        for p in range(N):
            a, q, w = boxes[p]
            if p >= k:
                cost = q + heap_sum
                profit = suf[p + 1] - cost
                if profit > ans:
                    ans = profit
            if k > 0:
                if len(heap) < k:
                    heapq.heappush(heap, -q)
                    heap_sum += q
                elif q < -heap[0]:
                    heap_sum += q - (-heap[0])
                    heapq.heapreplace(heap, -q)
        out.append(str(ans))
    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    solve()
```

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys
import heapq


def solve():
    input = sys.stdin.readline
    T = int(input())
    out = []

    for _ in range(T):
        N, M = map(int, input().split())

        if M == 1:
            ans = 0
            for _ in range(N):
                V, P = map(int, input().split())
                if V > P:
                    ans += V - P
            out.append(str(ans))
            continue

        if M > N:
            for _ in range(N):
                input()
            out.append("0")
            continue

        boxes = []
        for _ in range(N):
            V, P = map(int, input().split())
            a = V - 1
            q = P - 1
            w = V - P
            boxes.append((a, q, w))

        # Sort by remaining capacity descending.
        # For equal capacity, smaller cost first.
        boxes.sort(key=lambda x: (-x[0], x[1]))

        # suf[i] = sum of positive (V-P) in boxes[i:]
        suf = [0] * (N + 2)
        for i in range(N - 1, -1, -1):
            w = boxes[i][2]
            suf[i] = suf[i + 1] + (w if w > 0 else 0)

        # Need M-1 survivor boxes.
        # For a fixed last survivor position p, choose p and the
        # (M-2) smallest costs before p as survivors.
        k = M - 2
        heap = []  # max-heap by storing -q
        heap_sum = 0
        ans = 0

        for p in range(N):
            a, q, w = boxes[p]

            if p >= k:
                survivor_cost = q + heap_sum
                profit = suf[p + 1] - survivor_cost
                if profit > ans:
                    ans = profit

            if k > 0:
                if len(heap) < k:
                    heapq.heappush(heap, -q)
                    heap_sum += q
                elif q < -heap[0]:
                    heap_sum += q - (-heap[0])
                    heapq.heapreplace(heap, -q)

        out.append(str(ans))

    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))


if __name__ == "__main__":
    solve()
```
